Dejan Židan until 17:00, Branko Simonovic from 15 till 17:00, Ljudmila Novak, Natasa Sukić, Luka Mesec from 12.30 p.m., Violeta Tomić, Dr. Milan Brglez until 14 p.m. and from 17 to 19 p.m., mag. Branislav Rajić until 18:00, Janja Slug from 18:00 on, Jernej Vrtovac from 1350 to 16.15, Blaž Pavlin from 16:00 on, Dr. Franc Trček by 10 a.m., Victory Jelinčič Nobleni from 1:00 p.m., Gregor Perič to 11.30 p.m. and Lidija Divac Mirnik to 6 p.m. I say hello to everyone who's here! We're moving to nine. On the day-to-day basis, this is firstly a proposal for a law on the procedure of judicial protection of former holders of qualifying liabilities of banks. The draft law was tabled by the Government for consideration by the National Assembly. With regard to this bill, a group of 25 Members and Members with the first-signed Daniel Krivec requested that the National Assembly hold a general debate. In order to further explain the proposal, I give the floor to the representative of the Government, Mr Dragoň.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u2	Madam Vice President, honourable Members and Members, good morning! We are moving on to a challenging topic, which has been preparing for more than two years, related to subordinated obligations and the rights of former holders of qualified liabilities of banks. After years of economic and financial crisis, Slovenia was in a situation where Slovenian banks no longer had access to international financial markets and were no longer in a position to refinance their obligations both at home and abroad, without State aid or intervention. Bank ratings declined dramatically, partly as a result of psychosis, which was then in force and was present in international financial markets. The Bank of Slovenia assessed that the stability of the entire financial system was at risk and that the conditions for exceptional measures, as set out in the then applicable Banking Act ZBan-1, were met. In order to restore financial stability, monetary and executive authorities have proposed measures to remedy the banking system. Under the conditions permitted by European Union law at that time, the rehabilitation required the establishment of a new legal framework established by the amendment to the ZBan-1L Banking Act, which was established in November 2013. The new European rules on the use of public funds for the rehabilitation of banks required the burden-sharing principle, namely, the resolution of banks first from within, the so-called bail-in, before public funds in the form of State aid were engaged. The new legislation has provided that part of the financial burden of recovery is also borne by shareholders and holders of various subordinated and hybrid capital instruments, all of which have been designated by law as qualified liabilities by banks. These are all financial instruments that, by their nature, contribute to covering the losses of the bank. ZBan-1 has established rules under which Banka Slovenije has been able to impose extraordinary measures, including a measure to terminate qualified obligations. All the provisions of the Banking Act governing the implementation and enforcement of the measure terminating the qualifying obligations were subject to a constitutional judgment. By a decision of 2016, the Constitutional Court confirmed the constitutionality of the provisions on the basis of which exceptional measures were imposed and on the basis of which qualified obligations ceased. In doing so, the Constitutional Court also referred to the Court of Justice of the European Union and took into account judgment C526/14 in its decision and thus examined the compatibility of the law with the European legal order. In accordance with the Constitution, the Constitutional Court recognised 12 contested articles of the law, which constituted the basis for the extraordinary measures taken. This means, in particular, that the Constitutional Court confirmed that the Bank of Slovenia had in the contested regulation, in specific cases, the legal basis for the decision, which was challenged by the constitutional assessors. Whether the Bank of Slovenia has acted in accordance with this arrangement must be subject to judicial protection and will be verified in the manner proposed by this law. Due to the absence of specific procedural rules for the compensation disputes of former holders against Banka Slovenije, the Constitutional Court for Unconstitutional has recognised Article 350a of the Banking Act. In the Constitutional Decision, the emptiness established relates to the right of access to data, to the burden of proof and to the possibility of collective enforcement of judicial protection. These three themes are topics which are essentially covered by this law and which have been largely coordinated both inter-sectorally and publicly. The Court’s judgment will be based on the correctness of the exceptional measures carried out by the Bank of Slovenia and the eligibility to recover the damage. Any compensation to be paid to former holders may depend only on the correctness of the assets found at the time of the decision of the Bank of Slovenia. Undoubtedly, the holders had a significantly higher level of risk in these investments. However, it will be necessary to reassess whether Banka Slovenije correctly assessed the financial situation of banks during that period. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u4	Good-bye! Ladies and gentlemen, honourable Members! So today we have before us the proposal of the Government Act on the Judicial Protection Procedure of Former Holders of Qualified Liabilities of Banks. We have been in the media for several years to witness distorted stories about the backdrop of the rehabilitation of banks in 2013 and to see no end to them. It seems that the aim of all these contributions is to convince our citizens and taxpayers that there was no bank hole in Slovenia at all and that the tax money to be paid to banks has been collected by the European Union, the Bank of Slovenia and the Government. Such experiments divert attention from those who actually created the bank's hole by lending bad loans and are also responsible for them. The fact is, in the banking system, most of the bad credits were created between 2005 and 2007, and they are the responsibility of the bankers who were placed in the positions of the state banks by the then ruling politicians and who granted loans without adequate protection. Does anyone else deal with these credit agreements today? Are those responsible for the bank hole going to get an epilogue or a penalty? Will we leave the fate of Slovenia to them again, even though they have made major mistakes in the past? In the SAB Group, we are convinced that it is high time to spread the fog from this very difficult topic and explain to the citizens what speculation and deception are, but what are the facts. We want to present incontrovertible facts and say that the money was intended for the recapitalisation of banks and that it is still in banks. We want to continue to take care of the welfare of taxpayers, citizens who have by no means contributed to what has happened and those who for many years have been paying their taxes to the country, and ultimately do not get what belongs to them – these are our pensioners and pensioners. During the economic crisis, our banks and the country were on the brink of collapse, and Slovenia had only the following choice: to recapitalise banks with State aid or to leave banks in insolvency and risk bankruptcy and collapse not only of the financial system, but of the whole country. Slovenia had only money for a few months and could easily share the fate of Greece and some other European countries. These countries, which have received financial assistance and treatment by the so-called troika, are now far from Slovenia. Without true sovereignty and under strict control, what is happening and what is being done in these countries. Even for virtually every legal change, they still need to get a consent from outside. Unlike them, Slovenia then decided to fight, to overcome all its sovereignty and thus to ensure prosperity and further development. After 2013, economic growth, GDP levels, development and halting of austerity measures are irrefutable evidence that the government of Alenka Bratušek made the right decisions at the end of 2013. The government of Alenka Bratušek, as it was said, was then faced with the fact that banks without State aid in the form of recapitalisation would declare bankruptcy. In order to recapitalise banks with tax money, Slovenia needed the consent of the European Union – these are the rules. Under EU rules, public funds of taxpayers are not allowed to be allocated to rescue those shareholders and creditors who, even in the event of bankruptcy of the bank, would not receive anything, and I think this is perfectly correct. The European Union protects ordinary taxpayers' citizens. In a specific story, only a few percent of those people who bought these bonds through the window were banks, insurance companies, reinsurance companies and other so-called trained investors who were or should be aware of their risks. Slovenia is an integral part of the European Union and therefore we are obliged to respect its legislation. Let us not forget that we are creating it as citizens of the European Union with the influence on the European Members, and let us also consider together, on Sunday, whom we will surround and who will fight for our interests. Like other countries of the European Union, Slovenia has enacted a legal institution similar to a compulsory settlement. In 2013, it established the legal conditions under which Banka Slovenije led the valuation process of banks. If the Bank of Slovenia has demonstrated in this process that the financial situation of the bank is such that the bank is in imminent bankruptcy without recapitalisation, the Bank of Slovenia has issued extraordinary measures to relieve those who would not have received anything in the event of bankruptcy, including shareholders and bondholders. This is how Banka Slovenije acted in concrete cases. This law — and I am now going to the heart of it — has been given constitutional judgment. The Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia, as the highest authority of the judiciary and after consulting the Court of Justice of the European Union, has found that the provisions defining the legal conditions for the expression of these measures, which I am referring to, are the means by which banks can be rehabilitated in accordance with the constitutional and legal order of the Republic of Slovenia. I repeat, in accordance with the constitutional and legal order of the Republic of Slovenia; this has been established by the Constitutional Court on the way banks are being rehabilitated. As a non-constitutional point of view, it only recognized the provision on the judicial protection of minors, since it was not intended to provide real protection because they did not have sufficient access to information. The Constitutional Court has therefore ordered the legislator to arrange judicial proceedings by allowing the dischargeees to have access to all the financial and other information made available by the Bank of Slovenia at the time of the decision. On the basis of such data, they will be able to file an action under this law, which is before us today, in the event that Banka Slovenije has misjudged the financial situation of banks. If, in this process, where financial experts will cooperate, it is found that this situation has been found otherwise, the right is wrong, they will be entitled to a certain compensation, which is now legally – I think this remains in the proposal – limited to the contribution they have made. Why is the Bank of Slovenia and not the country paying compensation? In accordance with the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia and the Bank of Slovenia Act, the Bank of Slovenia is independent and independent in the performance of its tasks. It is not related to conclusions, views and instructions from State or other authorities. Thus, the Bank of Slovenia, in accordance with the constitutional basis of the law and its position, independently and independently gave the said measures; these exceptional measures, based on the provisions of the law, which the Constitutional Court considered to be constitutional. In the SAB party, we therefore welcome the proposal for a law that makes it easier and more effective to protect those who deserve judicial protection. We support all such solutions of a law that are just. We want the law to distinguish between so-called uneducated investors, which is to say those who were unaware of the risks and who were buying small-money bonds and those who are dealing with these transactions and who know the risks well – we are talking about so-called speculators, but here the speculator is not meant in a negative sense. A speculator is meant in terms of whether or not a business allows you to make a profit. In the process of adopting this law, we strongly opposed the legal solutions that would further burden the budget and taxpayers. We estimate that it is fair for those who have money – banks, insurance companies, reinsurance companies – as in all court proceedings, to bear the costs of experts and also the costs of judicial fees. Special protection should be given to small ones. Professional investors, such as insurance companies, pension companies, Kad, the Court of Justice and others whose investments were inadequately higher, including a few million, are liable to legal fees, as are all others, to be settled on their own in court proceedings. So let me point out the main facts in the end. If the Bank of Slovenia had no legal basis for assessing whether the banks were insolvent and would go bankrupt or not, and they were given extraordinary measures, the bank could not have solved the bankruptcy as the pillar of the banking system and the state without foreign aid. This legal basis was found by the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia to be in accordance with the Constitution and the legal order of the Republic of Slovenia. The Constitutional Court, in view of the imbalance of the parties — that is, the Bank of Slovenia as the defendants and investors — has assessed that the envisaged judicial protection is inadequate and that they need to be provided for judicial protection or have the opportunity to obtain more information. The last, strong economic growth, the rise in GDP, the end of austerity measures and the improvement of Slovenia's social well-being and reputation in the European Union is a sign that we did the right thing at the time and that the government of Alenka Bratušek made the right decisions in 2013. The decisions of the Constitutional Court are binding and must be respected and it is right to find the right solution in relation to judicial protection as well. As part of today's general debate, we will also open another open question in our party – and I hope that the rest – and perhaps even better solutions will be found. It may also be an open question that the Bank of Slovenia draws attention to. It is essential that the government law under consideration consistently governs what the Constitutional Court requires of the legislator. This shows that we respect the rule of law, as well as those who have acted in an unscrupulous manner. In the SAB Group, we believe that the proposed law is suitable for further consideration and we will also actively participate in further general discussion. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u6	Madam Vice-President of the National Assembly, Madam Members, Members of Parliament! The sovereignty of the State vis-à-vis the independence of the European banking institutions – that is precisely what is at the heart of the dispute in the recovery of banks, the deletion and confiscation of assets of shareholders and the deletion of holders of qualified liabilities. The implementation of stress tests, a careful review of banks' assets, the selection of valuations and the transfer of claims to a bad bank are business processes for which we cannot confirm compliance without the shadow of economic doubts and political and legal irresponsibility. The background shows at least three uncertainties: the first issue – the legality of these procedures, the second – the method of evaluation and the third – the scope of the new debt due to rehabilitation. The Act on the Judicial Protection of Former Holders of Qualified Liabilities of Banks should be adopted in the light of a decision of the Constitutional Court of 19 October 2016. The Constitutional Court ruled that article 350a of the Banking Act was inconsistent with the Constitution. This Article provided for the protection of shareholders and creditors in the event of an emergency decision. Due to the absence of specific procedural rules for the compensation disputes of former holders against the bank, non-constitutionality has been established with regard to the right of access to data, as regards the burden of proof and the ability of collective enforcement of judicial protection. The deadline for the adoption of the law expired on 15 May 2017. For Members of the Desus Group, it is essential that the proposal of the law takes account of the constitutional decision. The judicial protection of former holders of qualified liabilities of banks will thus be possible. The publication of decisions of the Bank of Slovenia has been determined by which exceptional measures have been taken and the way in which documents and data relating to emergency measures are provided. The legal arrangements for access to documents and data that have been taken into account by the Bank of Slovenia or should be taken into account in the statement of an exceptional measure to terminate the qualifying obligations of the bank, which was made under Article 261a of the Banking Act, are certainly necessary. Access will be enabled via the virtual data room. How, in what way, where, with what technical means, this virtual data room will be regulated, and above all, who will be the carrier, are the dilemmas that will have to be resolved in the next stages of the legislative process. The assessment of the political economic responsibility for the rehabilitation of banks is still an important Slovenian dispute. Without any doubt, the state and taxpayers carried a large part, the dominant part, the burden of bank recovery. On the basis of the need to maintain the business capacity of banks, at the end of 2013, the Government implemented an exceptional measure of termination or conversion of the qualifying liabilities of the Bank of Slovenia to the commercial bank, which provides for the contribution of owners and holders of subordinated instruments to cover the capital deficit. The capital owners were so disbursed that they themselves were unable to recapitalise the bank in order to ensure their business stability. Owners of subordinated obligations were also authorised. There are several claims: the banks have been overcapitalised, the owners of subordinated liabilities should not be divulged at all, not even the owners of the shares. It is not possible to judge the correctness of these claims today. It is important, however, that a legal possibility is given to all potential victims to obtain data and to decide on their content to take further action for action. In judicial proceedings, their claims will be confirmed or rejected. The law also lays down the reverse burden of proof, which means that the Bank of Slovenia will have to demonstrate the eligibility of the disqualification of holders of qualified obligations. In the event of a finding of overcompensation, however, the beneficiaries must be entitled to payment, according to the applicants. The defendant who will have to pay in these cases is the Bank of Slovenia. In the Desus Group, we believe that this is the case when it is found that the calculations were wrong as a basis for decommissioning. Whether the payment of any compensation by the Bank of Slovenia is a breach of the monetary financing rule is an issue on which the central bank justifies its opposition to the legal proposal in order to avoid its liability and potential financial obligations. The compensation paid, possibly paid, would not constitute financing the State's obligation, as public funds would not therefore be increased. And otherwise, ladies and gentlemen, what monetary financing is, is a matter of interpretation. For example, on the basis of the Banka Slovenije Act, the Bank of Slovenia has committed 25% of the surplus of expenditure in each business year to the budget of the Republic of Slovenia since June 2002, when this law was passed, never to be seen as contrary to the legal order of the European Union. Because if it were contrary to the legal order, Slovenia would never meet the pre-accession negotiations for entry into the European Union. Ladies and gentlemen! The proposal for a law is an opportunity that can restore the country's reputation. In the recovery processes, no one has ever resigned, no one has been on the defence of Slovenian state sovereignty, nor have the banks' administrations resigned because of the forced decommissioning of the committees. Managerial secrecy was no better than the political stability of the European Commission and the European Central Bank, and both undermined political confidence in the rule of law and in business banks, as well as of course in the central bank. Transparency here is the other side of responsibility and credibility is an integral part of the necessary integrity. In the Desus Group, we will support the law at first reading, but we are also prepared to support additional proposals to supplement the law that will reinforce or move towards the implementation of the Constitutional Court decision. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u10	Madam President, thank you for your floor. Dear Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues! We have before us the Law on the Judicial Protection of Former Holders of Qualified Liabilities of Banks. The Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia ruled that holders of deleted subordinated bonds had no effective judicial protection at the time of the significant deletion due to the bank hole in 2013 and that the Bank of Slovenia’s actions at the time were consistent with the Constitution. The Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia ruled that the amendment of the Banking Act, which allowed holders of subordinated bonds in Slovenian banks who had lost some 600 million euros in total, was partly unconstitutional in 2013. It was concluded that the holders of subordinated bonds had no effective judicial protection available and the decision was consistent with the Constitution in the work which allowed the Bank of Slovenia to act. Under the exceptional measure to close the qualifying liabilities of banks, in 2013, EUR 624 million of subordinated instruments and all equity in five banks, Nova Ljubljanska Banka, Nova Kredittna Banka Maribor, Abanka, Factor Banka and Probanka, and in one bank in 2014, in Banka Celje, together almost EUR 2 billion at book value. The Court of Justice has ordered the National Assembly to remove the anti-constitutionality within six months by allowing the exercise of the right of judicial protection for all possible actions already filed and future damages relating to the deletion. It also set a delay in the start of the expiry of claims for damages up to a six-month period starting after the annulment of the constitutionality established, which took place in October 2016. At that time, Finance Minister Matthew Vraničar Erman announced that the Government would follow the instructions of the Constitutional Court and prepare a proposal for legislative amendments. The former government, the Government of Mir Cerar, was therefore unable to follow the Constitutional Court and prepare a law on the procedure for the judicial protection of former holders of qualified obligations. This means that the Constitutional Court ordered the National Assembly to draw up, by about March 2017, such a law which would enable the holders of deleted bonds and shareholders to be effectively protected. There is no such thing in the National Assembly until today. One of the priority issues in the Slovenian Democratic Party's Parliamentary Group was the question to the Minister when he would prepare this law. So, after about one year of government action, we have this law on the table today. Sadly, he has been late for about three years with this law. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we are convinced that there are many serious and legitimate dilemmas regarding the procedure for determining the value of assets and the rehabilitation of banks in 2013, and we are convinced that the procedures are also being verified. The fact is, it has been eliminated among the most stringent in all of Europe. A number of comments were made at the new proposed law, which we received for consideration in the National Assembly, which is today the first reading, and a number of comments were actually made by all stakeholders involved in this law. This means those who have been deleted who assess that the Law on the Judicial Protection of Qualified Obligations Holders of Banks still does not eliminate unconstitutionality or fully comply with the requirements of the Constitutional Court. We have comments from the Bank of Slovenia, in particular there are different opinions about who will pay compensation in case the court so decides. In the Slovenian Democratic Party, we are aware of this importance and, in fact, the delay in the adoption of this law, so if the law follows the comments of the professional and interested public in the second reading and second reading, we will support such a law. We do not want to be the ones in the Slovenian Democratic Party who will make these processes difficult, and we will therefore not oppose the proposed law in today's vote on the first reading of this law. However, we predict in advance that in the event that the law remains in the same content as it is today, that is, where the comments of all stakeholders are actually – both those who have been deleted from this law and the Bank of Slovenia – we will not, of course, support the opinion of the European Central Bank in the second reading. Because we are convinced that such a law does not follow the Constitutional Court's decision. Nevertheless, the distinguished Secretary of State, colleagues and colleagues, in the Slovenian Democratic Party, we believe that the legislator will follow the comments of the professional public concerned and prepare a law that will enable effective judicial protection and create clear conditions for determining the value of compensation. So again, I repeat: we will not oppose today's first reading of the bill, which has also been imposed by the Constitutional Court. In the event that there are no changes and the comments of the professional and interested public are taken into account, we will certainly not support this law in the second reading.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u11	Thank you very much. The next position of the LMŠ Group will be presented by Mrs Andreja Zabret. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u12	Honourable Vice-President, Government representatives, Members and Members of Parliament! With the bill under consideration today, the coalition has received another unresolved problem at the table, which should have been resolved two years ago in 2017, but the previous government has failed to do so and another in a series of implementing decisions of the Constitutional Court, which have remained from previous or past governments. The former President of the Government resigned prematurely, the matter was not included in the Committee on Finance's meeting, and so all the coordination then fell into the water, and the issue was again addressed in the new configuration practically from the beginning. The Ministry of Finance has therefore drawn up a proposal for a law following thorough coordination with the interested and professional public and cross-cutting coordination. So let's start at the beginning. The bill refers to the issue of the rehabilitation of the Slovenian banking system and its associated bank deletions, which were carried out at the end of 2013 in NLB, Novi KBM and Abank and then at the end of 2014 Bank Celje. The aim of the bill is to legislate and ensure that former holders of qualified obligations are protected as effectively as possible in accordance with the Constitutional Court decision. The basic principle of the Banking Act-1, which was also correctly recognized by the Constitutional Court of the Republic of Slovenia, is that, as a result of the measure, no creditor of qualified liabilities should be in a worse position than if bankruptcy proceedings had been initiated over the bank. In the event of an extraordinary action, the Banking Act-1 provided for the judicial protection of shareholders and creditors of banks in the form of a compensation action brought against the Bank of Slovenia. Since the subject is very complex, allow me to define ourselves to the key solutions that are in the bill. The decision of the Constitutional Court requires us to establish a certain legal framework and that is a fact. It is worth noting that since the recapitalisation of the banks in December 2013, there have been constant doubts about its regularity and that the rehabilitation of the banks has not been carried out in a transparent and professional way, that it has been excessive and that the write-off of the bonds has not been justified. Whether this is true will be judged by the court. In the 2013 recovery assessment, there were some two opposite fields; on the one hand, members of a rather narrow group of people who were involved in the rehabilitation of banks at the time, of course, and on the other, a wider group, particularly beneficiaries and their lawyers, who pointed out all the irregularities that were to occur in the process of evaluating the bank portfolio and determining the capital adequacy of banks. Finally, we have the third largest and most vulnerable group of people who have lost their life savings because they have entrusted calls to purchase subordinate bonds and to the window vendors who were our banks. On the other hand, there were also well-informed financial investors, i.e. speculators who, prior to the rehabilitation of banks, bought subordinate bonds with a discount, counting on the profitability of the investment and then announced in the media that they had been unlawfully relaxed. If we start — if I may say so — with a solution that is most receptive in the public. If the court finds that the former holders are liable for compensation, the Bank of Slovenia will pay this to the extent that the reservations and general reservations are made in accordance with the Bank of Slovenia Act. If the compensation exceeds the amount of the reserves and general reserves, the difference of funds shall be provisionally borne from the budget of the Republic of Slovenia. In the Group of Members of the List of Marjan Šarca we do not agree with the statements of the Bank of Slovenia that in this case it is a monetary financing, as the public funding from this title would not increase. In our view, it is the financing of the victims and not the state. The draft law takes into account that the Bank of Slovenia has enacted compensation for former holders, according to which any compensation is paid by the Bank of Slovenia, and this has been duly confirmed by the Constitutional Court in its decision. It should be noted that the draft law does not lay down specific rules for judicial settlement. The legal argument is that legal parties, such as the Bank of Slovenia and former holders, have the possibility of concluding a settlement in accordance with the applicable legislation and the free will of the parties. In order to encourage the merger of former holders to file joint actions, it is laid down that a court fee shall be awarded only at the rate of 50%, which would be measured in accordance with the Law on Court Fees, provided that at least 30 former holders having a common authorisation are brought together. The court proceedings will be conducted in accordance with the rules of the fair procedure for economic disputes, and all proceedings will take place before the Maribor District Court. Here we allow the possibility that due to the very size of the process, which will be one of the largest in Slovenia, consideration will be given to the possibility of moving to Ljubljana. In order to find a solution to ensure proportionality between data protection and access to this data, a virtual data room with remote access using a qualified digital certificate will be provided in addition to the physical room to be established at the Ministry of Finance. It is extremely important for us in the Group of Members of the List of Marjan Šarc that only a real and former holder can bring an action if the qualified obligations were kept in a fiduciary account. In Slovenia we have decided on transparent ownership and let this be the case. In conclusion, it must be acknowledged that we all have a difficult task ahead of us. This is a new law, a law that regulates the issue of recapitalisation, which we met in Slovenia at the end of 2013 and we hope never again. The case is extremely complex, so we think it would be good for the courts to be able to clarify the fact of the group of experts from different fields. Of course, there are still some concerns – such as whether the proposed deadline for bringing the action is too short and what happens, for example, if experts in their opinion do not unite or when they should, but despite some minor concerns, that the proposal follows the decision of the Constitutional Court and we will therefore vote that it is appropriate for further consideration. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u13	Thank you very much. Mr Jani Prednik will present the position of the SD Group. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u14	Dear Vice-President, representatives of the Ministry, colleagues and colleagues, good afternoon! In 2013 and 2014, Slovenia tried to solve the huge problem that had arisen in its banks, avoiding the forced administration of the so-called troika and the actions of the International Monetary Fund, while maintaining a functioning economic system and maintaining financial sovereignty. In order to save the Slovenian banks, not only public funds which the State had to borrow in international markets were used, but the so-called Bail-in principle was used, whereby, in the event of the bank’s resolution, its owners had to take part in the bankruptcy. This included those who previously bought securities — known as subordinated liabilities — with which banks have been raising money for their capital adequacy since 2010. When it was found that deep holes were gaping in bank balance sheets, it was decided that the capital paid in subordinated bonds should also be used to cover these losses. At that time, the National Assembly adopted additions to banking legislation allowing Banka Slovenije to issue decisions allowing investors' investments to be fully devalued and converted to bank capital. Of course, it was a lot of money, which in total exceeds a billion euros. The holders of securities used a series of remedies and demanded a constitutional assessment of the legislation adopted. A number of arguments were cited in their favour. Among other things, they claimed that the depth of the bank hole was lower than was officially established and that the methodology used to determine the level of losses was not appropriate or used incorrectly. They also pointed out that they had not been properly informed of the risks and that the recovery thus carried out constituted an anti-constitutional intervention into their property rights. In 2016, the Constitutional Court decided that such a bank recovery had been carried out in accordance with the Slovenian Constitution and found that the bond holders had been violated the fundamental constitutional right to judicial protection. The legislation adopted did not allow them to challenge in court the decisions by which the Bank of Slovenia imposed on them the obligation to cooperate in the rehabilitation. Therefore, the Constitutional Court ordered the National Assembly to draw up a law allowing such judicial protection. We have such a proposal today before us after a proposal has already been tabled in the previous term, but due to early elections, the process of adoption has not been completed. Of course, the expectations of some for this law are high. Some feel that the law should return all their contributions to the injured because of the fact that it was a banking rehabilitation for crime, which had been delegated abroad. Others again consider that the State acted appropriately when the banks were rehabilitated, while others argue that there were no grounds for recovery. But this law is only about giving victims the opportunity to seek their right in court. Therefore, this proposal for a law establishes everything necessary for such procedures to finally run out. The following decision of the Constitutional Court provides for a way in which former holders of an obligation can prove their right. They will be able to do so if they can prove that banks would lose less in bankruptcy than they lost by participating in their resolution. Therefore, access to the data used to calculate the depth of the bank hole and which have never been disclosed to the public and not to the injured is essential. The bill thus establishes a way of accessing data through the data rooms, with a large part of the law designed to protect privacy and confidentiality in order to satisfy a number of rules that determine the independence of central banks and protect banking secrecy. The defendant is, under this law, the Bank of Slovenia, which, if its liability turns out to be, is also obliged to settle any damage. While it opposes this solution, the European Central Bank stands in the same position. They claim that it is a prohibited monetary financing, but we must remember the last years when both central banks referred to their independence every time they had to deposit their accounts. This independence was interpreted in such a way that they were not obliged to explain their conduct to anyone, as if their independence was a goal and not a means entrusted to them because of their important role and responsibilities toward people. We need you to remember that Slovenia has self-sustained its banks, so it did not have to call for the help of the troika. The Bank of Slovenia, which acted as a stand-alone regulator for the banking system in 2013 and 2014. From the very beginning, the Crown Act on Banka Slovenije provides that Banka Slovenije is responsible for the damage caused by its actions. Therefore, her indemnification does not interfere with her independence. In order to obtain compensation, we should be able to prove to the banks that there was no real risk of bankruptcy in those years. The possibility and platform for such proof gives this law to the affected. Today’s consideration of the law will surely raise many concerns and doubts, perhaps even unresolved questions. In the SD Group of Members, we believe that further consideration of the answers can be made, so we predict that, after today's debate, we will give support to the decision, giving a proposal for a law suitable for further consideration. Thank you.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u15	Thank you very much. The following is Monika Gregorčič, who will present the position of the SMC Group.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u16	Dear Vice President, Members and Members of Parliament, representatives of the Government! We have before us one of the more demanding legal materials of this mandate, which requires us to look at the whole picture. The law before us corrects the wrong decisions of the previous governments, which we know were not easy. The rehabilitation of the banking system has been indisputably professional, financially and legally extremely complex, and we can assume that the current government with the main actors in the banking recovery has certainly also faced political pressures, both internal and external, from the EU. For the time being, these pressures merely indicate certain media releases and the criminal charges filed by the National Investigation Office against the Bank of Slovenia's leadership at that time. Let me freshen up the memory of the situation. By 2013, the crisis had so endangered our banking system that it was threatened with so much inequity and insolvency and urgently needed fresh capital. In accordance with the recommendations of the European Commission, the Bank of Slovenia, in coordination with the Ministry of Finance, the European Commission, the European Central Bank and the European Banking Authority, through independent external advisory companies, launched in August 2013 quality reviews of the assets of banks and so-called stress tests. The results of the stress tests showed an expected capital deficit of almost EUR 5 billion, assuming an unfavourable scenario by 2015. The Slovenian banks therefore needed a substantial recapitalisation and, above all, immediate action. In accordance with European Union law, however, the consent to recapitalisation is granted by the European Commission to the interested private investor. The State recapitalisation constitutes a form of State aid which distorts competition substantially. Except under certain conditions, the European Commission may grant State aid only if it is appropriate, necessary, proportionate and provides for appropriate compensatory measures. Since August 2013, the European Commission has based its assessment of State aid authorisation on new rules, namely that State aid must not be granted to the bank in the event of a capital deficit until the subordinated instruments have been converted into equity. The National Assembly therefore adopted a government amendment to the Bank of Slovenia Banking Act in November 2013, allowing the Bank of Slovenia to provide legal basis for a measure to terminate the qualified liabilities of banks in difficulty. To put it simplistically, this law has allowed banks that no longer reach the minimum prescribed capital and are threatened with liquidation or bankruptcy and therefore need fresh capital to implement a measure of termination or conversion of qualified liabilities. In doing so, however, an individual creditor must not suffer greater losses than would have been the case if the bank had been bankrupt. The Constitutional Court then decided in its decision in October 2016 that the provision governing the legal protection of released securities holders, in breach of the Constitution and the National Assembly, imposed that the non-compliance should be abolished within a period of six months. Therefore, only a provision on legal protection was repealed, and not the measure itself of termination or conversion of qualified obligations. During the previous term of office, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia subsequently drafted and forwarded to Parliament the first draft of the law on the procedure for the judicial protection of holders of qualified liabilities of banks, which unfortunately did not wait for further consideration due to the conclusion of the mandate. It should be added that the current legislative proposal has substantially deviated from today's version. Today's legislative proposal refers to the 2016 Constitutional Decision in a way that allows released investors to sue Banka Slovenije for damages. This will be bound to pay compensation if it turns out that the assessment of the bank hole height was wrong, that is to say, too high. The following facts are specifically highlighted in the Group of the Modern Centre Party. As the bill is not fully harmonised, today we express only conditional support to it and expect the key open issues to be cleared below the legislative process, including the importance of the court's local competence in Maribor, the new duties of the Agency for the Market in Securities in Data Rooms, the suspension of the obligation to pay a court fee, the imposition of costs for an expert group. We would also like to hear the arguments of the Bank of Slovenia that propose certain changes, since the financial implications, namely the payment of interest and related costs, will be borne by the central bank. We hope that by the second reading of the draft law on the Committee on Finance, we will be able to draw up appropriate amendments with the aim of eventually adopting a law that is mostly close or coordinated with all key stakeholders. We certainly do not want to see a quick and unconceived law again subject to constitutional judgment. This would not be in the interest of anyone, even the least relaxed former owners, who have waited too long for their right. In particular, in the SMC Group, we draw attention to another very sensitive dimension of this issue. Who are the most important or most relaxed owners of subordinated securities. The most numerous groups are uneducated investors. In particular, these are natural persons who have been subordinated to bonds, in relatively small individual values, sold on bank boxes by bank officials, under the pretext that it is a safe investment, almost like deposits, but with a more favourable interest rate. These misleading investors already have a legal basis for the compensation actions of business banks for infringements of the Explanatory Obligation under the provisions of the Market in Financial Instruments Act. There is absolutely no need to wait for this law and to act earlier and to address the claim for compensation against the bank which has sold such securities to them. There is no need for such fraud of individual business banks to be paid by Banka Slovenije, a country or all taxpayers. Indemnity should be paid to informed uneducated investors by commercial banks that have violated all the rules of fair banking. However, this group of uneducated investors was obviously disappointed again. Under her wing, she was attracted by another, apparently by influence and financial power, a more important group of depraved people. These are well-educated, mostly institutional investors who were well aware of the advantages as well as the risks of investing in these securities. Some of them may be referred to as speculative investors. Some sources even suggest that the transaction with subordinated bonds is actually a scheme or a money-lending tool and that the sale was only a dot to untapped investors. We therefore welcome the special provision which provides for the disclosure of the actual identity of the former owners, since the filing of the actions will only be possible for them, but not for the guardians of the fiduciary accounts of hidden owners. We will support the law at this stage in the Group of the Modern Centre Party. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u18	Thank you for your word and a nice hello! The proposed law deals with issues of subordinated bond holders who were relieved by Banka Slovenije’s exceptional measure in 2013 in the process of bank rehabilitation. With the Bank of Slovenia’s exceptional measure, around 100,000 bank investors, subordinated bond holders and NLB, NKBM, Banks and other Slovenian banks were thus relieved. The total value of all the cancellations amounted to EUR 963 million, of which some EUR 600 million were subordinated. The owners are not only largely educated investors who knew that such bonds brought high returns, but also high risks, there are also largely uneducated small investors among investors who bought bonds on the basis of unfounded recommendations made by bank employees without greater awareness of the risks they were taking. In particular, on behalf of the latter, it is appropriate to ensure adequate judicial protection for the destitute. The recapitalisation of banks and the deletion of the owners of subordinated bonds have been surrounded by shadow of doubt for years. In particular, there is a doubt as to the appropriateness of the then valuation of the bank portfolio and the determination of the capital adequacy of the banks. It was on the basis of the contested valuation that there was a questionable need for large-scale recapitalisation of banks and, last but not least, for this extremely questionable write-off of subordinated bond owners. From the complaint of Slovenian criminalists against former Governor Boštjan Jazbec of Banka Slovenije, who has recently come to the public, it is quite clear that the entire process of correction from evaluation to deletion has been conducted inadvertently, unprofessionally and under severe political pressure from the European Commission and the European Central Bank. The Law on the Judicial Protection of Former Beneficiaries of Qualified Liabilities may be the first step on the road to reaching the bottom of this matter. Finally, the collective justice procedure of the declassified owners of the qualifying obligations against the Bank of Slovenia can be initiated. In the context of the procedure, it will be determined whether the damage caused by the effects of the Bank of Slovenia’s exceptional measure is higher than would have been the case if an exceptional measure had not been carried out. The answer to this question will depend in particular on the correctness of the valuation of funds and the assessment of the financial situation of banks in the recapitalisation phase. In the context of a fair procedure, there will also be a revision of the contested valuations of the bank hole. In Levi, we believe that the banking portfolio should be audited in accordance with the methods of the international accounting standard. Although the valuation of the bank portfolio was based on the so-called AQR method, it is feared that this inappropriate method has shown significant losses in banks and thus led to the deletion of subordinated bonds. It is clear from the complaint against Jazbec and the co-responsible that the leaders of the Bank of Slovenia, following the European Commission's dictatorship, allowed the publication of the results of an independent review of stress tests, although they were aware that these were not calculated in accordance with legally applicable international accounting standards. We therefore stress in Levica that the law should be supplemented by a provision that takes into account, in particular, the conformity of valuations with international accounting standards in the enforcement process. In Levica we welcome the provision of the law that compensation will be paid to beneficiaries primarily by Banka Slovenije, so that there will be no additional burden on taxpayers. The provisions of the Bank of Slovenia and the European Central Bank, which oppose the Bank of Slovenia covering this amount, are considered inadequate if it is to be an illicit monetary financing of the country. The Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union defines monetary financing as the financing of institutional units of the central regional or local level of the State and other persons of public law. In short, monetary financing is defined as public sector financing. However, the payment of compensation to the owners of subordinated bonds is not public sector financing and is therefore not contrary to the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union. It also seems unjustified to require the Bank of Slovenia to involve European institutions, in particular the European Commission and the European Central Bank. It is on the basis of the contested valuations of the bank hole that the European Commission has forced bank resolution based on the principle of bail-in, where not only shareholders but also owners of qualified bonds are relieved. It was the European Commission that imposed the decommissioning of the owners of subordinated bonds as a condition for the granting of State aid. For a long time, the European Central Bank has been hampering the investigation of the bank's resolution by political pressures and even by its judgments against the Republic of Slovenia and the domestic law enforcement authorities and the audit authorities. In short, both the European Commission and the European Central Bank have been putting pressure on the Slovenian State and its independent authorities for a long time and thus preventing, preventing, making it difficult to investigate, and should not participate in this process if it is to be independent. At this stage, we will support the proposal for a law in Levica, but we are subject to the second reading of the law by means of a proposed supplement, which is a provision under which the application of redress takes into account the conformity of valuations with international accounting standards. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u46	Thank you so much for saying that. Dear Mr Vice President, distinguished colleagues and colleagues! As we have heard many times today, the main objective of today's proposal for a law to remedy the non-compliance with the Constitution established by the Constitutional Court in its decision of 19 October 2016, namely the unconstitutional nature of certain provisions of the Banking Act which regulates the liability of the Bank of Slovenia for damage to the exceptional measures it has imposed. In the present judgment, the Constitutional Court confirmed that the legal basis for the exceptional measures imposed by the Bank of Slovenia in 2013 and 2014 in relation to the termination of subordinated instruments was indeed in line with the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia. At the same time, it decided that Article 350a of the Banking Act, which regulated judicial protection, was unconstitutional. The court proceedings under the relevant Article did not provide for effective judicial protection of former holders of qualified obligations of banks affected by the Bank of Slovenia’s exceptional measures. The substance of the judgment in question is, therefore, that the Constitutional Court has undoubtedly found that judicial protection was not effective because the law did not take into account and did not balance the unequal position of the investors of the subordinated bond compared to the Bank of Slovenia. It should then be pointed out that the Constitutional Court has de facto assessed whether the way in which banks were rescued by the removal of subordinates was in accordance with the Constitution. It is essential that the Constitutional Court’s substantive judgment largely states that the way banks were repaired by the removal of subordinates was in accordance with the Constitution. It should also be pointed out that the Constitutional Court has not assigned the subordinates that the deletion was illegal and that they are entitled to the refund of the lost money. Furthermore, the Constitutional Court has clearly found that the deletion did not violate the rule of law or the rule of law, so it did not violate Article 2 of the Constitution of the Republic of Slovenia. Why? Because subordinates were not in a worse position than creditors in bankruptcy proceedings. Therefore, they were in no way discriminated against and in an unequal position compared to creditors in bankruptcy proceedings. The removal was possible only if the State aid could prevent the bankruptcy of the bank and the threat of the financial system as a whole. The Regulation therefore clearly and definitively stated when and under what conditions the measure in question could be expressed, meaning that it was in accordance with the rule of law. However, the fact that the Constitutional Court has found that subordinates have been violated the right to an effective remedy provided by Article 23(2) of the Constitution. In spite of the fact that judicial protection was provided, they had the possibility of bringing damages against the Bank of Slovenia, and due to procedural shortcomings such as the absence of a special, rapid and economic procedure, collective judicial protection to ensure a uniform decision on disputes between subordinates and the Bank of Slovenia, such as long-term procedures, the lack of information regarding the assessment of the assets of banks and other data, which would allow the claimants to form an action and to manage the dispute, also due to the absence of specific procedural rules that would offset the professional and information imbalance between the Bank of Slovenia and subordinates, the demanding judicial protection was not effective. The proposed law abolishes all of the above, lays down specific procedural rules for the judicial protection of former holders of qualified obligations by banks which differ from the general rules and, inter alia, allow for the merger of judicial proceedings in which the parties are then in a similar situation for the joint and uniform treatment of actions relating to an individual bank which has been issued by the Bank of Slovenia with exceptional measures. The draft law specifically lays down rules for the two-step disclosure of documents and information relating to decisions on exceptional measures issued by the Bank of Slovenia. We have also heard that there is a reverse burden of proof; today there are many times. Finally, I think it is still necessary to recognise that it is legitimate, necessary and also lawful for the Bank of Slovenia to take responsibility and prove itself a defendant to have acted in accordance with its competences, and rightly so, after all, it was only the one who was liable for compensation. But I stress that being a defendant at the end of the day does not mean being guilty. It is therefore the responsibility of Banka Slovenije to prove otherwise, if not, and to repay the damage in full. For all these reasons, I will support the proposed law. Thank you.

Although the Bank of Slovenia and the European Central Bank are legally in breach of Article 123 of the Treaty on the Functioning of the European Union, which prohibits monetary financing for countries, which means that the profits of central banks from the issuance of money and the management of assets should be financed by the State, the following should be highlighted in this regard. This is not about monetary financing, since Banka Slovenije is responsible for its actions as regulator of the stability of financial institutions under Banka Slovenije Act 1L and the Banka Slovenije Act, and has given the measures independently and independently in accordance with the legal option given to it by the Banking Act. ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u51	Thank you very much. Member Jani Prednik has the floor and will be followed by Ambassador Maša Kociper. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u52	Thank you for your word. Have a nice hello again! It has been said several times in today's session that the Constitutional Court's judgment largely asserts that the way banks have been rehabilitated by the removal of subordinates in accordance with the Constitution, was inconsistent in only one part, namely that it was unconstitutionally declared only by the fact that the injured party was granted the right to judicial protection, but not in terms of effectiveness. Thus, the decision on deletion was in substance correct and, in one part, procedurally inappropriate, as it is said, judicial protection is not sufficiently effective. It is essential, however, that the Constitutional Court does not provide substantive proof to the subordinates that the deletion was illegal and that they are entitled to the refund of the lost money. What were the key findings of the Constitutional Court? Qualified liabilities subordinated to liabilities were not legally equivalent to the ordinary claims of the banks' creditors, since in the majority part they constituted the capital of the bank which was intended to cover the losses of the bank and to protect other creditors, in particular depositors. The deletion or cutting of subordinates was not contrary to the prohibition of retroactivity, as stated in Article 155 of the Constitution. There was no breach of the principle of trust in law, Article 2, of the Constitution, since it was only possible to delete it only if, with State aid, it was possible to prevent bankruptcy of the bank and the threat of the financial system as a whole. For the Constitutional Court, the decisive fact was in 2016 that the contested regulation contained the principle of no creditor at a disadvantage. Simply put, the subordinates lost no more than they lost in bankruptcy. The deletion did not prejudice the right to private property referred to in Article 33 in conjunction with Article 67 of the Constitution. However, a very important quote from the Constitutional Court’s judgment is: “The Constitution does not give rise to the duty of the State to reimburse the creditor through State aid money from private investment, which has proved to be economically unsuccessful.” So, as it is said, the only violation is thus a violation of the right to effective judicial protection referred to in Article 23, paragraph 2, of the Constitution. It was not enough for the subordinates to have the possibility of an indemnity action against the Bank of Slovenia. Protection should also be effective. So there's something wrong with the current system. In particular, firstly, the lack of information regarding the assessment of the value of the assets of banks and other data that would enable the claimants to form an action and to manage the dispute. Secondly, the absence of specific and adapted procedural rules that would offset the other professional and information imbalance between the Bank of Slovenia and its subordinates. Thirdly, the absence of specific, rapid and economic collective court proceedings to ensure quality and uniform decision-making in disputes between subordinates and the Bank of Slovenia. As it is said, in 2016 the Constitutional Court ordered the National Assembly to resolve this within six months. That did not work at the time, and this law now solves it, so it also has the support of the Social Democrats. Thank you very much.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u73	Thank you very much, proposer. In order to introduce the opinion, I give the floor to the representative of the Government, Mag. Aloys Stan. Here you go.
ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u74	Dear President, honourable Members! The Government of the Republic of Slovenia underlines its commitment to the early implementation of the legal provision concerning the establishment of the demographic reserve fund and the relevant activities are already being carried out at the Ministry of Finance. The demographic reserve fund will provide a sustainable and stable source of income for pension payments or reduce the pressure on the state budget's spending to change the demographic picture of the population. It is necessary to follow good practices of internationally comparable demographic reserve funds established for pre-financing of pension funds in accordance with the principles of security, liquidity, dispersion and return on investments. In the opinion of the Government of the Republic of Slovenia, the proposal for a law on the Slovenian Demographic Fund, submitted to the National Assembly by a group of Members and Members, does not follow these international practices. I'll try to explain why not. The transfer of ownership of all the country's strategic investments to the demographic fund prevents the targeted investment policy of the demographic fund, which must be based on the principles of security, dispersion, liquidity and return on investments. These principles cannot be pursued through the country's strategic investments, as strategic investments are aimed at achieving the country's strategic objectives and require active management in terms of supervision of the performance of companies, possible recapitalisations, reorganisations and restructurings, which is not the purpose of the demographic reserve fund. The function of the demographic reserve fund is to provide as high a return as possible for the sustainable and stable co-financing of the pension fund. The bill provides for the transformation of the Slovenian State Holding to the Slovenian Demographic Fund, which is contrary to the provisions of the Slovenian National Holding Act, which, in conjunction with the Pension and Disability Insurance Act, provides for the transformation of the capital company into a demographic reserve fund. In addition to this, an alternative capital investment management system is actually established, rather than a demographic fund. The draft law provides for the same objectives, principles, measures and management acts to ensure the transparency and efficiency of investment management as prescribed by the SDH Act, which is incomparable to a significantly more complex portfolio of investment by the demographic fund, which includes strategically important portfolio investments, real estate, bonds, claims and money market instruments. From the point of view of the Slovenian constitutional or legal order, the National Assembly cannot be the Assembly of the Slovenian Demographic Fund. The proposed structure of the Supervisory Board is inadequate as it poses a risk of political and other interest in the management of companies pursuing the country's strategic objectives. The assessment of the financial implications of the draft law on the State budget and other public funds is inadequate, since it does not include all aspects of the revenue and expenditure of the state budget. The expected time of accumulation of Slovenian Demographic Fund funds is inadequate or does not actually exist, as only half of the funds at the annual level are accumulated and the other half is immediately paid into the pension register. The proposal for a law cannot include tax provisions that the Slovenian Demographic Fund is not liable to corporate income tax, as tax treatment must be laid down in tax legislation, including the corporate income tax obligation. On the basis of the above proposal, the Government of the Republic of Slovenia does not support and considers that it is not suitable for further consideration. Thank you.

ParlaMint-SI_2019-05-21-SDZ8-Redna-08.ana.u76	Madam Vice-President of the National Assembly, honourable Members of Parliament, Members of Parliament! The demographic reserve fund is a necessity. On the income side of the Pension and Disability Insurance Office in the future, there will be no stable resources, both by content and by height. The revenue side with its own dynamics will not be able to follow the increasing cost needs. We must decide on an appropriate institutional solution to regulate the establishment of sustainable sources of financing, governance and quality future action. Under the provisions of the Slovenian State Holding Act, a capital company should be transformed into an independent autonomous demographic reserve fund on 31 December 2015. That didn't happen. During the previous term of office of the National Assembly, the Desus Group introduced a law on the Slovenian reserve demographic fund into the legislative procedure on 30 January 2018. The government withdrew a month and a half later, so the legislative process was closed. The dispersion between privatisation ambitions and the provision of a social state is the main cause of the inability to make a decision on this important institution. The logic of government property and its profitability prevails. It is clear that learning from mistakes in Slovenian political and corporate practices means repeating them, not eliminating them. Grotesque political complications, management farce and a tragic decline in management competences from the Slovenian State Holding and Bank Claims Management Company are up to some 10 influential companies, irrespective of political authority. Over the past nine years, four governments have repeated similar errors and the problems are clearly broader, systemic and long-term, linked to political culture and economic knowledge. Are we able to master them and achieve the goal of establishing a demographic reserve fund? Its fundamental purpose is to ensure the long-term sustainability of the pension system by providing certain pre-funded funds for the purpose of paying pensions. These funds will become part of the supplementary pension scheme after the end of their accumulation period. The main objective is to achieve a more balanced income of the pension fund for periods when expenditure is significantly above income. The way in which such a demographic fund operates pursues the objective of increasing assets and providing as high investment returns as possible to allow future pensions to be co-financed. The bill under consideration today aims to ensure that all state assets, strategic assets and portfolios are pooled together in only one legal form. The assumption is that, in this way, it would be more effective to provide for the financing of current and future pensions, the repayment of the country's debt and the Fund's funds could also be used to finance the expenditure of the State budget for other purposes. In this way, they will be transferred to the Strategic Investment Fund, where the State has to maintain 50% plus one share, significant investments of the country where the country has to maintain 25% plus one share, and portfolio investments of the country, capital company assets and consulting and management companies. This would transfer all state assets to the management of the Fund irrespective of the purpose for which the State disposes of it and manages it. Strategic investments are those with which the Republic of Slovenia, in addition to economic achievement, also achieves strategic objectives. The strategic objectives are related to the management of state infrastructure, public service objectives, security objectives, development objectives and other objectives that take account of important social interests. Important investments are those in which the Republic of Slovenia wants to preserve key development factors in the country. The Fund will not actively manage these types of investments. This means that he will not sell or purchase them and will not be able to achieve the basic objective of the demographic fund, achieving as high a return as possible. It will, however, assume obligations to cover potential losses and liabilities for the potential recapitalisation of these companies. All of this from financial resources for the additional charging of the pension fund. The idea of transforming the Slovenian state holding company into a demographic fund contains an important and difficult to overcome internal conflict. It is reflected in the conflict between the development interests of fund-owned enterprises and their financing and the provision of a high capital and dividend return to finance future pensions. The question raised is not only hypothetical – will the Fund, for example, finance the recapitalisation of the Slovenian Power Plant Holding, as long as it is needed for the sake of poor business, literally on the shoulders of pensioners, their future pensions? In the Desus Group we advocate the transformation of the capital company into a demographic fund because the capital company of pension insurance was established in 1996 precisely to provide additional funds for the pension fund. What is missing today is additional, by height and structure stable resources. In recent years, Desus has taken care of one of the sources of funding. In the Slovenian State Holding Act of March 2014, we achieved that 10% of purchases are made from the sale of state assets for the financing of the demographic fund. To date, this account has raised € 103 million of earmarked money, earmarked funds for the transfer to the demographic fund. The exchange of assets between the Slovenian State Holding Company, the State and the Capital Company will certainly have to be made, so that the capital company in its portfolio will have all the assets that will enable it to achieve the goal of maximising the yields for future co-financing of pension funds. The volume of assets will need to be increased, which means that it will have to be accumulated first over a specified period without any other use. No accumulation period is foreseen in the proposal. The financial resources are expected to be used to finance the current needs of the pension fund, to repay the country's debt and to fund the current replenishment. The potential conflict will arise between the State's necessary debt reduction funds and the necessary accumulation funds in line with, on the one hand, the financing of pension liabilities and the risks to financial resolution of fund-owned enterprises that will be in difficulty on the other hand. The risk of achieving the highest possible yield is thus seen as uncontrollable. However, this does not mean that more pension funds are now available because the level of public spending is limited on the basis of a fiscal rule. The authorities of management and control are established in a classical way — the Assembly, the Supervisory Board and the Administration. However, it is a pretty original idea that the legislative branch of power plays the dominant role in the formation of the Assembly and the Supervisory Council compared to the executive branch. From the point of view of the operationality of the action and the decision-making of the Assembly, and in particular from the point of view of the functionality of the work of the Supervisory Board, the proposal is likely to imply the inability of timely and correct decision-making and the risk of irrational bureaucratic tactics with adverse consequences. The executive power, the Government, must play a decisive, decisive role in such connotations. Today’s drafters copied about four fifths of the text under consideration from a law that he drafted and submitted to Desus about 15 months ago. We would expect this to be a sufficient reason to support the law, but Members of the Desus Group cannot do so. The conceptual differences in the size of the assets transferred to the fund in terms of the design of the management bodies and the accumulation age are simply too great. We respect the efforts and, above all, appreciate the work that has been put forward by the applicants, and we have not overlooked their delicate concern that they have identified the acronyms of the future fund by their own parties according to their ideas. It's not undetected. Thank you very much.
